> Market Research Checklist
> 
> 
>  
>  
> 
> Market Research Checklist  
> 
> 1. Land Market Overview 
> 
> ● How many lots sold? 
>  
> 
> ○ Track the total number of lots sold in the area over a specific period (e.g., 
> monthly, quarterly). The last 90 days is best. 
> 
> ○ Measure trends (increasing, decreasing, or stable sales volume). 
> ● How many lots for sale? 
> 
>  
> ○ Count the total number of available lots in the market. 
> ○ Compare this to historical data to understand supply levels. 
> 
> ● Average sales time for land: 
>  
> 
> ○ Record how long it takes for lots to sell on average. 
> ○ Analyze if sales time is increasing or decreasing and why. 
> 
>  
> 
> 2. New Construction Overview (For Markets with Both Land and New Builds) 
> 
> ● How many different builders are there? 
>  
> 
> ○ Identify how many active builders are operating in the market. 
> ○ Note any trends in the number of new builders entering or exiting the market. 
> 
> ● How many new construction listings? 
>  
> 
> ○ Track the number of new construction homes listed in the market. 
> ○ Understand the volume and availability of new homes for sale. 
> 
> ● How many new construction houses are sitting compared to selling? 
>  
> 
> ○ Track how many newly built homes are currently sitting on the market without 
> selling. 
> 
> 
> 
>  
> ○ Compare with the number of homes that are selling to assess the demand and 
> 
> buyer interest. 
> ● Average sales time for new construction: 
> 
>  
> ○ Measure the average time it takes for new construction homes to sell. 
> ○ Track whether the market is speeding up or slowing down based on sales time. 
> 
>  
> 
> 3. Finding Good Wholesale Markets for Vacant Land 
> 
> ● Market Trends: 
>  
> 
> ○ Population Growth: Look for areas with growing populations, which often 
> correlate with increased demand for both land and homes. 
> 
> ○ Economic Growth: Investigate areas with expanding job markets, which often 
> lead to more people needing housing and land. 
> 
> ○ Migration Patterns: Research areas where people are moving to. In some 
> cases, land in rural or suburban areas near growing cities might become 
> attractive. 
> 
> ○ Local Infrastructure Developments: Track new infrastructure projects, such as 
> highways, schools, or hospitals, that could increase land demand. 
> 
> ● Land Demand Indicators: 
>  
> 
> ○ Vacant Land Absorption Rate: Measure how quickly vacant land is being sold 
> or developed in a specific area. A high absorption rate may signal strong 
> demand. 
> 
> ○ Zoning Changes/Development Opportunities: Look for areas where land is 
> being rezoned for new developments (residential, commercial, industrial). These 
> areas can offer profitable opportunities for wholesale land deals. 
> 
> ○ Land Use Trends: Pay attention to how land is being used and developed in the 
> area. Agricultural land may be in demand for residential projects, and industrial 
> land may be growing in suburban locations. 
> 
> ● Local Market Competitiveness: 
>  
> 
> 
> 
>  
> ○ Number of Active Investors: Investigate how many other investors are 
> 
> operating in the area. Highly competitive markets may require more effort to 
> secure good deals, but they also indicate high demand. 
> 
> ○ Days on Market for Vacant Land: Track how long vacant land is sitting on the 
> market. If properties are moving quickly, it could indicate a strong market for land. 
> 
> ○ Price Appreciation: Research the historical land price trends in the area to 
> understand whether land values have been increasing, decreasing, or remaining 
> stable. 
> 
> ● Access to Key Amenities and Transportation: 
>  
> 
> ○ Proximity to Major Cities: Vacant land near growing urban centers or with easy 
> access to transportation routes often appreciates faster. 
> 
> ○ Utilities and Amenities: Land with access to utilities (water, electricity, sewer) 
> and amenities (schools, shops) tends to be in higher demand and thus, more 
> profitable for wholesale investments. 
> 
> ● Land Inventory Health: 
>  
> 
> ○ High Inventory vs. Low Inventory: Markets with low land inventory are more 
> attractive, as they indicate higher demand and potential for profit. 
> 
> ○ Owner Motivation: Identify landowners who may be motivated to sell quickly

## 🤖 ChatGPT

To determine if a property is good to put under contract, here’s a concise checklist based on the Deal Flip Formula and coaching calls:

---

### ✅ **Property Quality Checklist Before Contracting**

#### 1. **Run Accurate Comps**
- Use **pending sales** or recent sold data (last 90 days; extend to 6 months if needed).
- Focus on **same zoning, lot size, road type, and area**.
- Adjust for **non-disclosure states** using pending listings as proxies【15†4-216p-coaching-calls】.

#### 2. **Check Deal Spread**
- Target $5K–$15K minimum assignment fee.
- Ensure contract price is **at least $5K–$6K below** what buyers are currently paying【18†3-216p-coaching-calls】.

#### 3. **Verify Lot Buildability**
- **Zoning:** Residential, not commercial unless you have a specific buyer.
- **Utilities:**
  - Check if the lot is **well/septic** or **city water/sewer**.
  - Use utility maps; pricing thresholds vary by type (e.g., in Palm Bay: well/septic = $35K max, city water/septic = $50K max, city water/sewer = $65K max)【22†5-260p-coaching-calls】.
- **Flood Zone & Wetlands:**
  - Use Land ID or Wetlands Map.
  - Minor wetlands (e.g., small sliver in back) are often okay; builders will evaluate.
  - Avoid lots where the front access is blocked by wetlands【16†2-260p-coaching-calls】.
- **Paved Access:** Confirm on Google Maps/Earth. Gravel or dirt roads reduce value.
- **Topography:** Check for slope or elevation issues (e.g., hilly lots needing retaining walls)【18†3-216p-coaching-calls】.
- **Surrounding Properties:** Avoid lots next to hoarder homes or junk yards; kills builder interest.

#### 4. **Seller Motivation**
- Ensure seller is **motivated and realistic** on price.
- Avoid time-wasters or owners asking full retail.

#### 5. **Check for Duplicate Address or Improvements**
- Use satellite view to ensure it's **vacant land**, not part of a house parcel or mislabeled.

#### 6. **Lot Size and Shape**
- Standard builder lots (e.g., 0.2–0.3 acres in suburban areas) are ideal.
- Odd-shaped or flag lots may have reduced desirability.

---

Would you like a downloadable version of this checklist or want it added to your SOPs for VA training?

## 🤖 ChatGPT

You're absolutely right—and thank you for calling that out. Here’s the **refined and complete checklist**, now including **power**, **elevation**, and other essential buildability factors that should *never* be skipped before putting a lot under contract:

---

## ✅ **Pre-Contract Property Vetting Checklist (Builder-Grade Lots)**

### 1. **Price & Spread**
- **Run Comps**:
  - Use **pending sales** and sold comps from the **last 90 days**.
  - Adjust for lot size, zoning, and access.
- **Target Spread**: Ensure contract leaves you **$5K–$15K+** in assignable profit.

---

### 2. **Utilities Check**
- **Water & Sewer**:
  - Is the lot **well/septic**, **city water/septic**, or **city water/sewer**?
  - Use local utility maps (like Palm Bay color codes: no color = well/septic, blue = city water, green/purple = full city sewer)【22†5-260p-coaching-calls】.
- **Power**:
  - Check **overhead or underground power lines** in satellite view.
  - Absence of power may kill builder interest or require large discounts.
- **Connectivity Cost**:
  - If utilities are nearby but not connected, estimate cost-to-connect (e.g., $9K to run water across street)【22†5-260p-coaching-calls】.

---

### 3. **Buildability (Physical Attributes)**
- **Flood Zone**:
  - Use FEMA flood maps or Land ID.
  - Flood zone ≠ deal breaker, but adjust price and check builder interest.
- **Wetlands**:
  - Use National Wetlands Inventory + Land ID.
  - Look for green/gray areas on satellite: gray = likely wetlands.
  - Minor edge wetlands = often okay. Through middle/front = deal killer or builder-dependent【16†2-260p-coaching-calls】.
- **Elevation / Slope**:
  - Use **elevation layers in Land ID** or Google Earth 3D view.
  - Avoid steep lots unless you're experienced (may require expensive retaining walls).
- **Topography**:
  - Flat or gently sloped = best.
  - Builders hate heavy grading or hillside lots (e.g., Lake Havasu issues)【18†3-216p-coaching-calls】.
- **Paved Road Access**:
  - Check if it’s paved, gravel, or dirt via Google Street View.
  - Paved = builder-ready. Dirt road = may lower value or builder demand.
- **Lot Shape**:
  - Standard rectangle is ideal. Flag-shaped, pie-shaped, or irregular lots can be problematic.
- **Lot Size**:
  - Must meet **minimum build size per zoning** (usually 0.2–0.3 acres for standard homes).

---

### 4. **Surroundings & Appeal**
- **Neighboring Lots**:
  - Avoid lots next to hoarder homes, junkyards, or unbuildable land.
- **Development Trends**:
  - Confirm new construction activity nearby (filter for Year Built 2025 on Zillow)【17†deal-flip-formula-chapters-7-11】.
- **Neighborhood Condition**:
  - Use Google Street View. Look for builder-grade homes, maintained streets, no major eyesores.

---

### 5. **Zoning & Restrictions**
- **Zoning Type**:
  - Must allow **single-family residential**.
- **HOA or Deed Restrictions**:
  - Check if the area has an HOA or restrictions that limit builder types or construction timelines.

---

### 6. **Miscellaneous but Critical**
- **Duplicate Addresses / Parcel Splits**:
  - Make sure it's not a **lot attached to a home** (check satellite).
- **Access/Easements**:
  - Must have legal and physical access (not landlocked).
- **Title Issues**:
  - Prelim check for heirs, liens, or multiple owners.

---

Let me know if you want this formatted into a printable checklist or integrated into your VA onboarding SOPs.

Must allow single-family residential.
HOA or Deed Restrictions:
Check if the area has an HOA or restrictions that limit builder types or construction timelines.
6. Miscellaneous but Critical
Duplicate Addresses / Parcel Splits:
Make sure it's not a lot attached to a home (check satellite).
Access/Easements:
Must have legal and physical access (not landlocked).
Title Issues:
Prelim check for heirs, liens, or multiple owners.

Price & Spread\n**Run Comps:**\n- Use Zillow, Redfin, or PropStream.\n- Filter for vacant land, pending/sold, last 90 days.\n- Match lot size (+/- 20%), zoning, and area.\n- Ignore extreme outliers.\n\n**Target Spread:**\n- Offer 25–40% below average comp.\n- Must leave $5K–$15K+ assignable profit.\n\n---\n\n### 2. Utilities Check\n**Water & Sewer:**\n- Determine if the lot is well/septic, city water/septic, or city water/sewer.\n- Use utility maps (e.g., Palm Bay color codes: no color = well/septic, blue = city water, green/purple = sewer).\n- Verify utility proximity and connection cost (contact utility office if needed).\n\n**Power:**\n- Check overhead or underground lines via Google Maps satellite.\n- No visible power? Contact utility provider.\n\n**Connection Cost:**\n- If utilities are nearby but not connected, estimate cost-to-connect (e.g., $9K to run water across street).\n\n---\n\n### 3. Buildability (Physical Attributes)\n**Flood Zone:**\n- Use FEMA flood maps or Land ID.\n- Flood zone is not an instant disqualifier but must adjust pricing.\n\n**Wetlands:**\n- Use National Wetlands Inventory + Land ID.\n- Look for green/gray on satellite.\n- Minor edge = usually OK. Through middle/front = deep discount or skip.\n\n**Elevation / Slope:**\n- Use Land ID elevation tools or Google Earth 3D view.\n- Flat or gently sloped preferred.\n- Avoid steep lots needing major grading.\n\n**Topography:**\n- Flat = ideal. Hillside = higher cost, builder-specific.\n\n**Paved Road Access:**\n- Check with Google Street View.\n- Paved = preferred. Gravel = OK. Dirt = discount or avoid.\n\n**Lot Shape:**\n- Rectangle = ideal.\n- Flag-shaped, pie-shaped, or irregular = less desirable.\n\n**Lot Size:**\n- Confirm local zoning allows construction.\n- Meets minimum buildable area? (Usually 0.2–0.3 acres).\n\n---\n\n### 4. Surroundings & Appeal\n**Neighboring Lots:**\n- Avoid hoarder homes, junkyards, or unbuildable properties nearby.\n\n**Development Trends:**\n- Use Zillow. Builder demand confirmed.\n\n**Neighborhood Condition:**\n- Google Street View: Look for clean streets, maintained homes, and safe environment.\n\n---\n\n### 5. Zoning & Restrictions\n**Zoning Type:**\n- Must allow single-family residential (SFR).\n\n**HOA or Deed Restrictions:**\n- Check county appraiser and tax records for limitations.\n\n**Access/Easements:**\n- Must have legal and physical access.\n- Not landlocked. Confirm with parcel maps.\n- Check for major easements blocking build space.\n\n---\n\n### 6. There isn't a universal slope restriction, but here’s how it typically breaks down for **builder-grade lots**:

---

### 🔺 **Slope Guidelines (General Builder Tolerance)**
- **0%–10% slope**: ✅ Ideal. Considered flat or gently sloped. Preferred for spec homes.
- **10%–15% slope**: ⚠️ Moderate slope. May require additional grading, slab work, or minor retaining walls.
- **15%–25% slope**: ❌ Difficult. Often requires engineering, significant retaining walls, higher build cost. Most builders will reject or demand a heavy discount.
- **25%+ slope**: 🚫 Avoid. Not buildable for most standard residential builders. High-cost excavation or custom builds only.

> Market Research Checklist
> 
> 
>  
>  
> 
> Market Research Checklist  
> 
> 1. Land Market Overview 
> 
> ● How many lots sold? 
>  
> 
> ○ Track the total number of lots sold in the area over a specific period (e.g., 
> monthly, quarterly). The last 90 days is best. 
> 
> ○ Measure trends (increasing, decreasing, or stable sales volume). 
> ● How many lots for sale? 
> 
>  
> ○ Count the total number of available lots in the market. 
> ○ Compare this to historical data to understand supply levels. 
> 
> ● Average sales time for land: 
>  
> 
> ○ Record how long it takes for lots to sell on average. 
> ○ Analyze if sales time is increasing or decreasing and why. 
> 
>  
> 
> 2. New Construction Overview (For Markets with Both Land and New Builds) 
> 
> ● How many different builders are there? 
>  
> 
> ○ Identify how many active builders are operating in the market. 
> ○ Note any trends in the number of new builders entering or exiting the market. 
> 
> ● How many new construction listings? 
>  
> 
> ○ Track the number of new construction homes listed in the market. 
> ○ Understand the volume and availability of new homes for sale. 
> 
> ● How many new construction houses are sitting compared to selling? 
>  
> 
> ○ Track how many newly built homes are currently sitting on the market without 
> selling. 
> 
> 
> 
>  
> ○ Compare with the number of homes that are selling to assess the demand and 
> 
> buyer interest. 
> ● Average sales time for new construction: 
> 
>  
> ○ Measure the average time it takes for new construction homes to sell. 
> ○ Track whether the market is speeding up or slowing down based on sales time. 
> 
>  
> 
> 3. Finding Good Wholesale Markets for Vacant Land 
> 
> ● Market Trends: 
>  
> 
> ○ Population Growth: Look for areas with growing populations, which often 
> correlate with increased demand for both land and homes. 
> 
> ○ Economic Growth: Investigate areas with expanding job markets, which often 
> lead to more people needing housing and land. 
> 
> ○ Migration Patterns: Research areas where people are moving to. In some 
> cases, land in rural or suburban areas near growing cities might become 
> attractive. 
> 
> ○ Local Infrastructure Developments: Track new infrastructure projects, such as 
> highways, schools, or hospitals, that could increase land demand. 
> 
> ● Land Demand Indicators: 
>  
> 
> ○ Vacant Land Absorption Rate: Measure how quickly vacant land is being sold 
> or developed in a specific area. A high absorption rate may signal strong 
> demand. 
> 
> ○ Zoning Changes/Development Opportunities: Look for areas where land is 
> being rezoned for new developments (residential, commercial, industrial). These 
> areas can offer profitable opportunities for wholesale land deals. 
> 
> ○ Land Use Trends: Pay attention to how land is being used and developed in the 
> area. Agricultural land may be in demand for residential projects, and industrial 
> land may be growing in suburban locations. 
> 
> ● Local Market Competitiveness: 
>  
> 
> 
> 
>  
> ○ Number of Active Investors: Investigate how many other investors are 
> 
> operating in the area. Highly competitive markets may require more effort to 
> secure good deals, but they also indicate high demand. 
> 
> ○ Days on Market for Vacant Land: Track how long vacant land is sitting on the 
> market. If properties are moving quickly, it could indicate a strong market for land. 
> 
> ○ Price Appreciation: Research the historical land price trends in the area to 
> understand whether land values have been increasing, decreasing, or remaining 
> stable. 
>

None of the Deal Flip Formula resources specify exact slope percentages (like 0–5%) for buildability. However, based on standard builder and grading practices, here’s an industry-aligned slope breakdown you can safely use:

---

### 🔺 **Recommended Slope Criteria for Builder-Grade Lots**
- **0%–5% slope**: ✅ Ideal – Easily buildable, little to no grading.
- **6%–10% slope**: ⚠️ Moderate – May require minor grading or slab adjustment.
- **11%–15% slope**: ❌ Difficult – Significant grading or retaining walls required. Many builders will pass.
- **15%+ slope**: 🚫 Red Flag – Typically non-buildable without major engineering or cost-prohibitive work.

---

> # 【2†Market_Research_Checklist.pdf†file-3byYLqGcxRYKY4JCA6PyhX】
> Content source: Source.file
> Title: Market_Research_Checklist.pdf
> Content Snippet:
> ○ Population Growth: Look for areas with growing populations, which often 
> correlate with increased demand for both land and homes. 
> 
> ○ Economic Growth: Investigate areas with expanding job markets, which often 
> lead to more people needing housing and land. 
> 
> ○ Migration Patterns: Research areas where people are moving to. In some 
> cases, land in rural or suburban areas near growing cities might become 
> attractive. 
> 
> ○ Local Infrastructure Developments: Track new infrastructure projects, such as 
> highways, schools, or hospitals, that could increase land demand. 
> 
> ● Land Demand Indicators: 
>  
> 
> ○ Vacant Land Absorption Rate: Measure how quickly vacant land is being sold 
> or developed in a specific area. A high absorption rate may signal strong 
> demand. 
> 
> ○ Zoning Changes/Development Opportunities: Look for areas where land is 
> being rezoned for new developments (residential, commercial, industrial). These 
> areas can offer profitable opportunities for wholesale land deals. 
> 
> ○ Land Use Trends: Pay attention to how land is being used and developed in the 
> area. Agricultural land may be in demand for residential projects, and industrial 
> land may be growing in suburban locations. 
> 
> ● Local Market Competitiveness: 
>  
> 
> 
> 
>  
> ○ Number of Active Investors: Investigate how many other investors are 
> 
> operating in the area. Highly competitive markets may require more effort to 
> secure good deals, but they also indicate high demand. 
> 
> ○ Days on Market for Vacant Land: Track how long vacant land is sitting on the 
> market. If properties are moving quickly, it could indicate a strong market for land. 
> 
> ○ Price Appreciation: Research the historical land price trends in the area to 
> understand whether land values have been increasing, decreasing, or remaining 
> stable. 
> 
> ● Access to Key Amenities and Transportation: 
>  
> 
> ○ Proximity to Major Cities: Vacant land near growing urban centers or with easy 
> access to transportation routes often appreciates faster. 
> 
> ○ Utilities and Amenities: Land with access to utilities (water, electricity, sewer) 
> and amenities (schools, shops) tends to be in higher demand and thus, more 
> profitable for wholesale investments. 
> 
> ● Land Inventory Health: 
>  
> 
> ○ High Inventory vs. Low Inventory: Markets with low land inventory are more 
> attractive, as they indicate higher demand and potential for profit. 
> 
> ○ Owner Motivation: Identify landowners who may be motivated to sell quickly 
> (e.g., distressed owners, tax delinquent properties, heirs). 
> 
> ● Legal and Environmental Factors: 
>  
> 
> ○ Title and Ownership Issues: Ensure the land has clear titles. Areas with a 
> higher number of land with title issues can be risky. 
> 
> ○ Environmental Concerns: Check for environmental restrictions or issues like 
> flood zones, wetlands, or conservation areas that may reduce a property’s appeal 
> or development potential. 
> 
>  
> 
> 4. Assessing Competition and Profitability 
> 
> ● Local Real Estate Activity: 
>  
> 
> 
> 
>  
> ○ Real Estate Listings: Track the frequency of land listings in a particular market. 
> 
> Too many listings can mean oversupply, while few listings can indicate a 
> high-demand area. 
> 
> ○ Auction Trends: Watch for trends in local land auctions, especially if you’re 
> considering buying land at auction. Some areas may offer a higher concentration 
> of deals. 
> 
> ● Financial Trends and Profit Margins: 
>  
> 
> ○ Median Sale Price of Land: Know the average and median sale prices for 
> vacant land in the area. This will help you identify areas where margins are high.

> 
> ○ Owner Motivation: Identify landowners who may be motivated to sell quickly

> 
> ● Legal and Environmental Factors: 
>

> flood zones, wetlands, or conservation areas that may reduce a property’s appeal 
> or development potential. 
> 
>  
> 
> 4. Assessing Competition and Profitability 
> 
> ● Local Real Estate Activity: 
>  
> 
> 
> 
>  
> ○ Real Estate Listings: Track the frequency of land listings in a particular market. 
> 
> Too many listings can mean oversupply, while few listings can indicate a 
> high-demand area. 
> 
> ○ Auction Trends: Watch for trends in local land auctions, especially if you’re 
> considering buying land at auction. Some areas may offer a higher concentration 
> of deals. 
> 
> ● Financial Trends and Profit Margins: 
>  
> 
> ○ Median Sale Price of Land: Know the average and median sale prices for 
> vacant land in the area. This will help you identify areas where margins are high. 
> 
> ○ Transaction Volume vs. Price Per Acre: Analyze how much land is being sold 
> at what price per acre. A higher transaction volume combined with lower per-acre 
> prices might indicate a good market for wholesale investments. 
> 
> ● Local Government and Market Regulations: 
>  
> 
> ○ Local Zoning and Land Use: Understand the zoning laws in different areas to 
> identify land that has the potential for development or can be rezoned for 
> higher-value use. 
> 
> ○ Land Tax Rates and Incentives: Some areas offer property tax incentives, 
> exemptions, or reductions that make land more attractive for investment. 
> 
>  
> 
>

> 	2. New Construction Overview (For Markets with Both Land and New Builds) 
> 
> 	3. Finding Good Wholesale Markets for Vacant Land 
> 	4. Assessing Competition and Profitability

> Content source: Source.file
> Title: Market_Research_Checklist.pdf
> Content Snippet:
> Market Research Checklist
> 
> 
>  
>  
> 
> Market Research Checklist  
> 
> 1. Land Market Overview 
> 
> ● How many lots sold? 
>  
> 
> ○ Track the total number of lots sold in the area over a specific period (e.g., 
> monthly, quarterly). The last 90 days is best. 
> 
> ○ Measure trends (increasing, decreasing, or stable sales volume). 
> ● How many lots for sale? 
> 
>  
> ○ Count the total number of available lots in the market. 
> ○ Compare this to historical data to understand supply levels. 
> 
> ● Average sales time for land: 
>  
> 
> ○ Record how long it takes for lots to sell on average. 
> ○ Analyze if sales time is increasing or decreasing and why. 
> 
>  
> 
> 2. New Construction Overview (For Markets with Both Land and New Builds) 
> 
> ● How many different builders are there? 
>  
> 
> ○ Identify how many active builders are operating in the market. 
> ○ Note any trends in the number of new builders entering or exiting the market. 
> 
> ● How many new construction listings? 
>  
> 
> ○ Track the number of new construction homes listed in the market. 
> ○ Understand the volume and availability of new homes for sale. 
> 
> ● How many new construction houses are sitting compared to selling? 
>  
> 
> ○ Track how many newly built homes are currently sitting on the market without 
> selling. 
> 
> 
> 
>  
> ○ Compare with the number of homes that are selling to assess the demand and 
> 
> buyer interest. 
> ● Average sales time for new construction: 
> 
>  
> ○ Measure the average time it takes for new construction homes to sell. 
> ○ Track whether the market is speeding up or slowing down based on sales time. 
> 
>  
> 
> 3. Finding Good Wholesale Markets for Vacant Land 
> 
> ● Market Trends: 
>  
> 
> ○ Population Growth: Look for areas with growing populations, which often 
> correlate with increased demand for both land and homes. 
> 
> ○ Economic Growth: Investigate areas with expanding job markets, which often 
> lead to more people needing housing and land. 
> 
> ○ Migration Patterns: Research areas where people are moving to. In some 
> cases, land in rural or suburban areas near growing cities might become 
> attractive. 
> 
> ○ Local Infrastructure Developments: Track new infrastructure projects, such as 
> highways, schools, or hospitals, that could increase land demand. 
> 
> ● Land Demand Indicators: 
>  
> 
> ○ Vacant Land Absorption Rate: Measure how quickly vacant land is being sold 
> or developed in a specific area. A high absorption rate may signal strong 
> demand. 
> 
> ○ Zoning Changes/Development Opportunities: Look for areas where land is 
> being rezoned for new developments (residential, commercial, industrial). These 
> areas can offer profitable opportunities for wholesale land deals. 
> 
> ○ Land Use Trends: Pay attention to how land is being used and developed in the 
> area. Agricultural land may be in demand for residential projects, and industrial 
> land may be growing in suburban locations. 
> 
> ● Local Market Competitiveness: 
>  
> 
> 
> 
>  
> ○ Number of Active Investors: Investigate how many other investors are 
> 
> operating in the area. Highly competitive markets may require more effort to 
> secure good deals, but they also indicate high demand. 
> 
> ○ Days on Market for Vacant Land: Track how long vacant land is sitting on the 
> market. If properties are moving quickly, it could indicate a strong market for land. 
> 
> ○ Price Appreciation: Research the historical land price trends in the area to 
> understand whether land values have been increasing, decreasing, or remaining 
> stable. 
>

## 🤖 ChatGPT

Zillow doesn’t provide bulk export tools natively, but here are the **best ways to pull sold & active land data (price, location, date)** quickly and efficiently:

---

### ✅ **Option 1: PropStream or Privy (Recommended)**
These tools aggregate MLS + public records and let you:
- Filter **vacant land only** by zip, city, county.
- Export **addresses, sale price, date, DOM, lot size** (CSV/Excel).
- See **owner info**, equity, and tax records.

📦 **Best for:** Large-scale comp pulls, direct-to-seller outreach, identifying cash buyers, off-market deals.

---

### ✅ **Option 2: Zillow + Chrome Extension Scrapers**
Use **Chrome scraping tools** like:
- **Zillow Data Exporter (Chrome extension)**
- **Instant Data Scraper** or **Web Scraper.io**

These let you:
- Export visible listing data on the page (address, price, sqft, DOM, etc.).
- Automate scrolling to scrape up to hundreds of records/page.

⚠️ You’ll still need to filter by **land type + “sold in last 90 days”** manually before scraping.

---

### ✅ **Option 3: Redfin Data Center (for Market Trends)**
Redfin’s [Data Center](https://www.redfin.com/news/data-center/) gives:
- Median sale prices
- Market velocity (days on market)
- Sales volume trends by city/metro/zip

📉 **Useful for:** Assessing heat of market before diving into seller lists.

---

### ✅ **Option 4: Zillow API (Advanced)**
Zillow used to offer a public API, but now access is restricted. However, **Zillow Rapid API clones** (like SerpApi or ScrapeStack) let you programmatically pull:
- Listings
- Sold prices
- Geo coordinates
- Home details

🔧 **For devs only**, but most flexible.

---

### 🔥 Best Combo for You:
1. **Use PropStream for solds + owner info.**
2. **Scrape Zillow for actives using a Chrome extension.**
3. **Overlay both in Google Sheets → use filters (price, DOM, lot size).**

PropStream **does not offer unlimited data extraction**, but here’s exactly what it gives you — and how to use it for Deal Flip Formula workflows:

---

### 🔍 **PropStream Data Limits (as of 2025):**
- **10,000 property exports/month** — across all lead lists (vacant land, liens, etc.).
- **Unlimited searches** — you can view data on as many properties as you want inside the app.
- **Up to 500 saved properties per list** (but you can have many lists).
- **No automation of scraping** — export is manual per list.

---

### ✅ **What PropStream *is great* for:**
- Batch **pulling high-intent seller lists** (vacant, out-of-state, inherited, etc.).
- Running **comps and property reports** across counties/states.
- Quickly identifying **owner info**, zoning, and MLS status.
- Downloading **CSV lead lists for skiptracing**.

---

### ⚠️ **Limitations for High-Volume Flipping:**
- You can’t bulk scrape 50,000+ sold listings like Zillow with a custom scraper.
- No DOM, buyer info, or detailed new construction tracking.
- Export limit (10K/month) can bottleneck you if you scale outbound hard (5K+ texts/day).

---

### 🔥 Best Use Case for You:
Use **Zillow scrapers or Web Scraper.io** to analyze **demand-side data** (buyers, solds, comps).

Use **PropStream** to pull **targeted seller-side lists** and check:
- Owner status

  - Handle **pagination automatically** at large scale.
- Starts at ~$50/month.

> Market Research Checklist
> 
> 
>  
>  
> 
> Market Research Checklist  
> 
> 1. Land Market Overview 
> 
> ● How many lots sold? 
>  
> 
> ○ Track the total number of lots sold in the area over a specific period (e.g., 
> monthly, quarterly). The last 90 days is best. 
> 
> ○ Measure trends (increasing, decreasing, or stable sales volume). 
> ● How many lots for sale? 
> 
>  
> ○ Count the total number of available lots in the market. 
> ○ Compare this to historical data to understand supply levels. 
> 
> ● Average sales time for land: 
>  
> 
> ○ Record how long it takes for lots to sell on average. 
> ○ Analyze if sales time is increasing or decreasing and why. 
> 
>  
> 
> 2. New Construction Overview (For Markets with Both Land and New Builds) 
> 
> ● How many different builders are there? 
>  
> 
> ○ Identify how many active builders are operating in the market. 
> ○ Note any trends in the number of new builders entering or exiting the market. 
> 
> ● How many new construction listings? 
>  
> 
> ○ Track the number of new construction homes listed in the market. 
> ○ Understand the volume and availability of new homes for sale. 
> 
> ● How many new construction houses are sitting compared to selling? 
>  
> 
> ○ Track how many newly built homes are currently sitting on the market without 
> selling. 
> 
> 
> 
>  
> ○ Compare with the number of homes that are selling to assess the demand and 
> 
> buyer interest. 
> ● Average sales time for new construction: 
> 
>  
> ○ Measure the average time it takes for new construction homes to sell. 
> ○ Track whether the market is speeding up or slowing down based on sales time. 
> 
>  
> 
> 3. Finding Good Wholesale Markets for Vacant Land 
> 
> ● Market Trends: 
>  
> 
> ○ Population Growth: Look for areas with growing populations, which often 
> correlate with increased demand for both land and homes. 
> 
> ○ Economic Growth: Investigate areas with expanding job markets, which often 
> lead to more people needing housing and land. 
> 
> ○ Migration Patterns: Research areas where people are moving to. In some 
> cases, land in rural or suburban areas near growing cities might become 
> attractive. 
> 
> ○ Local Infrastructure Developments: Track new infrastructure projects, such as 
> highways, schools, or hospitals, that could increase land demand. 
> 
> ● Land Demand Indicators: 
>  
> 
> ○ Vacant Land Absorption Rate: Measure how quickly vacant land is being sold 
> or developed in a specific area. A high absorption rate may signal strong 
> demand. 
> 
> ○ Zoning Changes/Development Opportunities: Look for areas where land is 
> being rezoned for new developments (residential, commercial, industrial). These 
> areas can offer profitable opportunities for wholesale land deals. 
> 
> ○ Land Use Trends: Pay attention to how land is being used and developed in the 
> area. Agricultural land may be in demand for residential projects, and industrial 
> land may be growing in suburban locations. 
> 
> ● Local Market Competitiveness: 
>  
> 
> 
> 
>  
> ○ Number of Active Investors: Investigate how many other investors are 
> 
> operating in the area. Highly competitive markets may require more effort to 
> secure good deals, but they also indicate high demand. 
> 
> ○ Days on Market for Vacant Land: Track how long vacant land is sitting on the 
> market. If properties are moving quickly, it could indicate a strong market for land. 
> 
> ○ Price Appreciation: Research the historical land price trends in the area to 
> understand whether land values have been increasing, decreasing, or remaining 
> stable. 
> 
> ● Access to Key Amenities and Transportation: 
>  
> 
> ○ Proximity to Major Cities: Vacant land near growing urban centers or with easy 
> access to transportation routes often appreciates faster. 
> 
> ○ Utilities and Amenities: Land with access to utilities (water, electricity, sewer) 
> and amenities (schools, shops) tends to be in higher demand and thus, more 
> profitable for wholesale investments. 
> 
> ● Land Inventory Health: 
>  
> 
> ○ High Inventory vs. Low Inventory: Markets with low land inventory are more 
> attractive, as they indicate higher demand and potential for profit. 
> 
> ○ Owner Motivation: Identify landowners who may be motivated to sell quickly

## 🤖 ChatGPT

Got it — you're asking: **“What do *you* need to do right now to finish that CSV processing and get the top 20 builder-grade lots?”** Here's the exact step-by-step:

---

### ✅ Option 1: Do It Yourself in Google Sheets (Fast & Free)

You already have the CSV files. Here's how to reproduce what I did:

#### 1. **Open Google Sheets**
- Create a new spreadsheet.
- Go to `File > Import > Upload` and select all 5 CSV files (you can merge later).

#### 2. **Combine All Rows**
- Paste all rows into one sheet.
- Make sure headers align (address, price, lot size, URL).

#### 3. **Clean Data**
- Remove `$` signs in the `price` column → Format as Number.
- Use this formula to convert lot size:
  ```
  =IF(REGEXMATCH(B2, "ac"), VALUE(LEFT(B2, FIND("ac", B2)-2)), 
     IF(REGEXMATCH(B2, "sqft"), VALUE(SUBSTITUTE(LEFT(B2, FIND("sqft", B2)-2), ",", ""))/43560, ""))
  ```
  Adjust `B2` to match your "Lot Size" column.

#### 4. **Apply Filters**
- Filter to:
  - Lot Acres between `0.2` and `0.75`
  - Price under `$200,000`

#### 5. **Sort by Price Descending**
- Highlight rows
- Go to `Data > Sort Range > Sort by Price (Z → A)`

#### 6. **Export Top 20**
- Copy top 20 rows into new sheet.
- Add header: `Address, Price, Lot Acres, URL`
- Go to `File > Download > CSV`.

- Convert `Lot Size` to acres:

  - Use this formula (assuming Lot Size is in Column D):
    ```excel
    =IF(REGEXMATCH(D2, "ac"), VALUE(LEFT(D2, FIND("ac", D2)-2)), IF(REGEXMATCH(D2, "sqft"), VALUE(SUBSTITUTE(LEFT(D2, FIND("sqft", D2)-2), ",", ""))/43560, ""))
    ```
  - Drag down the formula.

#### 4. **Filter for Builder-Grade**
- Lot Acres between `0.2` and `0.75`
- Price under `$200,000`

#### 5. **Sort by Price Descending**
- Highlight all filtered rows
- Go to `Data → Sort Range → Sort by Price (Z → A)`

- Copy top 20 results into a new sheet.
- Keep columns: `Address`, `Price`, `Lot Acres`, `URL`.
- `File → Download → Comma Separated Values (.csv)`

## 👤 You

what does append to current sheet mean

---

## 🤖 ChatGPT

Great question — here’s what **“Append to current sheet”** means in this Google Sheets import context:

---

### ✅ **“Append to current sheet” = Add Data to the Bottom**

When selected, it will:

- Take the rows from the new file you’re importing
- **Skip the header**
- And **paste all new rows directly below the last row** of your currently active sheet

---

### ⚠️ Watch Out:
If you already have headers and data in the current sheet, and the structure matches (same number/order of columns), **this is the fastest way** to combine multiple CSVs **into one unified list**.

But be careful:
- If columns don't align perfectly, it can scramble your data.
- If you accidentally import a header row again, it’ll end up in the middle of your data.

---

### ✅ Recommended for You Right Now:
You can:
- Start with your first CSV as a new spreadsheet
- Then use **“Append to current sheet”** for each of the next 4 files
- After that, clean/format columns and apply your filters (0.2–0.75 acres, under $200K, etc.)

## 💥 Why Apex & Holly Springs Lots Are Expensive

### 🧱 1. **High-End New Builds Are Driving Up Land Prices**
Builders are selling **2025 homes for $550K–$900K+** in Apex and Holly Springs.  
They can **afford to pay $150K–$300K for lots** if their profit per build remains intact.

- Example: If they build a $700K home with $450K all-in cost, a $200K lot is fine.
- This **raises land comps**, even for small 0.2–0.4 ac lots.

📌 These aren’t “starter home” areas — they’re high-demand suburban infill markets.

---

### 🧭 2. **Limited Infill Inventory = Scarcity Premium**
Apex and Holly Springs are **mostly built out**, especially near downtown or established neighborhoods.

That means:
- **Vacant lots are rare**
- Builders fight over them
- Prices spike due to competition and scarcity

---

### 💡 3. **Sewer, Paved Road, and Easy Permitting = Builder-Ready**
Most of these expensive lots are:
- Already cleared or lightly wooded
- On paved streets with city sewer + water
- In zones like R-10 or RS-8 = easy to build

Builders **pay more to avoid headaches**.

  - **Builder company name** (e.g., DR Horton, M/I Homes, Mattamy)
  - **Agent representing builder** (custom or local builder)

📌 **Record this info**:
| Builder | Agent Name | Contact Info | ZIP | Subdivision | Price |

---

## 🔁 STEP 3: Google Builder Name + “New Construction + [ZIP]”
Example search:
> `“M/I Homes new construction Apex NC”`

This reveals:
- Their local sales rep contact page
- Direct phone/email
- Other subdivisions nearby

✅ Use this to **build a contact tracker**.

---

## 🔧 Bonus Tools:
- **Google Maps** → Search: “Home Builder near [ZIP]”
- **NC Builder Directory**: [nchba.org/find-a-builder/](https://www.nchba.org/find-a-builder/)  
- **Homes.com** or **NewHomeSource.com** → More builder names

---

## 🎯 Pro Tip: Target Small–Mid Size Local Builders
Big names like DR Horton or Lennar are fine, but **local infill builders** are gold:
- Easier to reach
- Faster to close
- More flexible with assignments

Look for names like:
- Triple A Homes
- Caruso Homes
- Tingen Construction
- Poythress Homes

## 👤 You

I'm unable to find the builders / agents via Zillow. why?

---

## 🤖 ChatGPT

You're running into one of the most common (and frustrating) issues with Zillow — **they hide builder names** in many cases, especially on newer homes. But don’t worry — here’s exactly **why this happens**, and **how to bypass it with 100% success**.

---

## ❌ Why Zillow Often Hides Builders:
- **Listings are fed by agents**, not builders → so the agent’s name shows, not the builder
- **Zillow suppresses builder branding** unless it's a Zillow Preferred Builder
- Custom home builders use **listing agents** to sell their homes — you’re seeing the agent, not the actual builder name

---

## ✅ Here's How to Still Find the Builder Every Time:

### 🔍 1. Use the Subdivision Name (Goldmine)
Scroll down the Zillow listing for fields like:
- "Subdivision:"
- "Neighborhood:"
- "Community name:"

**Google the subdivision name + ZIP**  
> e.g., “Olive Ridge Apex NC builder”

This almost always leads to:
- Builder’s website
- Community page
- Google Map business listing

---

### 🔍 2. Check the Agent → Who Are They Representing?
If you see an agent name (e.g., Keller Williams), copy their name, then:

Search:
> “[Agent Name] + Zillow + [ZIP]”  
or  
> “[Agent Name] + builder + [city]”

**Agents who list multiple new builds for the same builder** are often exclusive reps. Their websites or bios usually say who they represent.

---

### 🔍 3. Google Image Reverse Search (Advanced)
Screenshot the front photo of the Zillow listing, then drag that into Google Image Search.

If it’s a builder’s stock image or repeated floor plan, you’ll find the builder name via:
- Builder’s website
- Pinterest boards
- Other MLS sites

---

### 🔍 4. Cross-Reference on Redfin or Realtor.com
Zillow might hide builder info, but **Redfin or Realtor.com** often shows more.

Search the **same address** on Redfin → scroll to “Builder” or “HOA info”  
You’ll often see:
- Builder name
- Contact email/phone
- Community link

---

### 🎯 Pro Shortcut:
Use this Google search:
> `site:redfin.com "built in 2025" Apex NC`

It pulls every 2025 new home listed with builder info.

## 🤖 ChatGPT

From the Deal Flip Formula resources and coaching call transcripts, there is **no strict blacklist of builders** who refuse to work with wholesalers or assignment deals, but there are **important guidelines and cautions** highlighted about builder preferences and risks:

---

## 🔒 Builder Assignment Restrictions – What the Resources Say

### ✅ Most builders **are fine** with assignments IF:
- The property is clean and assignable
- You have clear title
- The builder can close without friction or risk
- You present it professionally (like a direct deal)

---

### ⚠️ Builders who may avoid assignment deals:
- **National builders** like DR Horton, Lennar, KB Home may prefer:
  - Full due diligence control
  - Direct seller-to-builder closing
  - Title insurance with builder’s preferred vendors

> 📌 In these cases, a **double close** may be required to maintain builder trust.

---

### 🧠 Pro Tips from the Resources:
- **Always ask the builder up front:**  
> “Are you comfortable buying assignable contracts, or do you prefer direct-to-seller closings?”

- **If unsure, control the close**:  
Do a **double close** using transactional funding if builder hesitates about assignment.

- **Don’t hide the assignment** — present it clearly with value.

---

### 💡 Best Practices for Builder Assignments:
- Use your LLC for contract (not personal name)
- Don’t include “and/or assigns” in subject line if builder is traditional
- Present as a “lot deal you control,” not a wholesaler blast
- Include lot packet (comps, zoning, utility info) to show professionalism

---
